#### Corals

The primary purpose of this report is to provide a quantitative biological resource assessment of Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary so that changes in time can be quantitatively documented in comparison with this baseline information.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
Towards this objective, we have presented summary statistics for each reef-building (hermatypic) coral species at each depth along each permanent transect for Fagatele Bay in Tables 3a-y.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
Likewise, we have provided summary statistics for each hermatypic coral species at the reef margin and at 6 m depth for April 1982 and for April 1985 at 12 additional locations around Tutuila Island (Tables 4-15).

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
In Fagatele Bay, the percent substrate coverage by hermatypic corals (Table 16) tended to be greatest in areas with strong wave action, e.g., coverage by corals generally decreased along a gradient from transects in shallow areas to transects in deeper waters and the outer Transects 1 and 6 had greater coverage by corals than did the central transects at comparable depths.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
Although these trends can be seen in Table 16, statistical demonstration of these trends can not be given because of a large interaction term brought about by the presence of a large clump of Echinopora hirsutissima at 9 m near Transect 2.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
We believe this general trend is probably a result of protection of corals from Acanthaster planci by water surge and wave action.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
A. planci does not maintain its position on the substrate in areas of strong surge.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Echinopora hirsutissima is not a favored prey of A. planci and this may be why such a large patch was present at 9 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
We looked for patterns in the distribution of favored prey (Acroporidae) and disfavored prey (e.g., Poritidae, Mussidae, Milleporidae), in Table 3, but in all cases there were strong interaction terms which prevented generalizations.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
The abundance of

hermatypic corals in Fagatele Bay was patchy and no clear patterns could be observed (Table 17).

--- SENTENCE 739 ---
This may result from aggregated and irregular recruitment.

--- SENTENCE 740 ---
Likewise, because of interactions, there were no clear patterns in the distributions of mean colony sizes (Table 18), coral community diversity values, degrees of evenness of representation of coral species, or degree to which the coral communities were dominated by a few species (Table 19).

--- SENTENCE 741 ---
Nevertheless, with the summary statistics obtained in this study, a comparison can be made with statistics from future studies to see if a significant change has occurred.

--- SENTENCE 742 ---
Data from the survey indicate that the coral community in Fagatele Bay is a diverse and complex system.

--- SENTENCE 743 ---
Frequent interactions obscure general patterns.

--- SENTENCE 744 ---
The present distribution of corals in the community may result in part from differential predation pressure from <a href="Acanthaster planci">Acanthaster planci</a> in 1979 as well as from aggregated and irregular patterns of coral recruitment.

--- SENTENCE 745 ---
The purpose of this survey is to provide a basis for a quantitative comparison of future changes and to allow statistical analysis of these changes in community structure.

--- SENTENCE 746 ---
For convenience, a complete list of all species of scleractinian corals observed on each transect at each depth in Fagatele Bay is given in Appendix 1.

--- SENTENCE 747 ---
A complete list of species observed at 12 locations around Tutuila outside Fagatele Bay in 1979, 1982 and 1985 is given in Appendix 2.

####

--- SENTENCE 748 ---
Macroinvertebrates

The densities of macroinvertebrates along 19 transects in Fagatele Bay are presented in Table 20.

--- SENTENCE 749 ---
A qualitative assessment of macroinvertebrates observed on the reef outside the areas censused on the transects is presented in Table 21.

--- SENTENCE 750 ---
Echinoderms were the predominant benthic invertebrates, occurring on all transects.

--- SENTENCE 751 ---
Observed on 95% of the transects, Echinometra mathaei was the most common of the five species of urchins encountered.

--- SENTENCE 752 ---
E. mathaei reached its greatest abundance in the more sheltered reef of Transects 2 and 3, where it inhabited the interstices of dead branching corals.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
In the more wave-assaulted zones of Transects 1 and 6, it occupied a system of grooves in the reef pavement.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
Conversely, the burrowing urchin <u>Echinostrephus</u> sp. was more abundant in the higher energy environments where reef pavement was covered by a thin sand and algal turf.

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
This occurred at a mean density of  $236.6/m^2$  along the 15-ft (5 m) isobath of Transect 1.

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
At the 30-ft (9 m) isobath of Transect 1 and the 25-ft (7.6 m) isobath of Transect 6, it attained mean densities of  $109.7/m^2$  and  $102.9/m^2$ , respectively.

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
Other species of echinoids were neither as common nor as abundant as <u>E</u>.

<u>mathaei</u> or <u>Echinostrephus</u> sp. <u>Echinothrix</u> <u>diadema</u> was found in the two shallower zones of Transects 2, 4, and 6.

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
Less common were <u>Eucidaris</u> <u>metularia</u> and <u>Diadema</u> sp., which occupied the deeper isobaths.

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
The small coral reef asteroid <u>Linckia multifora</u> was the only starfish occurring on transects.

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
This species was most commonly observed on the deeper isobaths where it was sheltered from wave surge.

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
Only two individual <u>Acanthaster planci</u> were observed in Fagatele Bay during our studies in April 1985.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
The soft coral <u>Sinularia</u> sp. also formed a conspicuous component of the macroinvertebrate fauna.

--- SENTENCE 763 ---
Present on 14 transects, colonies of <u>Sinularia</u> sp. were more abundant in shallow areas of moderate to high wave and current activity.

--- SENTENCE 764 ---
A single colony of <u>Sarcophyton</u> sp. was encountered on the transect at the 40-ft isobath of Transect 1.

--- SENTENCE 765 ---
The highest diversity among the macrobenthos was exhibited by the gastropod assemblage.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
Fourteen species of neogastropods occurred on the transects, representing about 78% of all species of snails.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
Only two species each of archeogastropods and mesogastropods were found on the transects.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
The predominance of neogastropods was also reflected in population densities; 71% of the gastropod fauna consisted of neogastropods, while archeogastropods and mesogastropods made up 11% and 3% of the fauna, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
The most common and most abundant of the neogastropods was the muricid Morula uva, followed by the fasciolariid Peristernia fastigium.

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
A single species of opisthobranch gastropod was observed on the transects.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
However, this species, the nudibranch <u>Phyllidia</u> sp., constituted about 14% of all gastropods present.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
Thus, its abundance was almost equal to that of the archeogastropods and mesogastropods combined.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
Although a number of dead bivalves was observed in Fagetele Bay (Table 21), the giant clam <u>Tridacna maxima</u> was the only live species encountered on transects.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
This probably represents a conservative estimate of relative abundance of bivalves because there was not sufficient time to examine habitats of the cryptic or the infaunal species, such as <u>Arca avellana</u> and Scutarcopagia scobinata.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
The principal crustaceans inhabiting the transected areas of the reef were hermit crabs of the family Diogenidae.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
These crustaceans were collected and preserved for later identification by Roy K. Kropp of the University of Maryland.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
A medium-sized parthenopid crab, <u>Daldorfia</u> cf.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
<u>horrida</u>, was observed adjacent to the 10-ft (3 m) isobath on Transect 3.

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
A preliminary list of gastropods collected or observed at 11 sites around Tutuila is given in Table 22.

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
Further study is being given to crustaceans, asteroids, and ophiuroids to provide accurate identification of these taxa.

--- SENTENCE 781 ---
Macroinvertebrate specimens collected during this survey are being compiled into a reference collection to be deposited at the Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary headquarters in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 782 ---
Where available, duplicate specimens will be deposited at the University of Guam Marine Laboratory.

--- SENTENCE 783 ---
As noted by Eldredge (1979), few marine faunistic surveys have been conducted in American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 784 ---
Previous studies of Fagatele Bay have encompassed fish surveys (Wass 1978) and general reef mapping (U.S. Army Corps of Engineers 1980).

--- SENTENCE 785 ---
Unfortunately, few details of the composition of the macroinvertebrate community have been provided.

--- SENTENCE 786 ---
Data from the present study indicate that the macroinvertebrate fauna of Fagatele Bay is representative of the Indo-West Pacific region.

--- SENTENCE 787 ---
We collected two of the 13 species of asteroids known from Samoa (Marsh 1974) at Fagatele Bay, and three additional species were found outside the sanctuary.

--- SENTENCE 788 ---
Although no ophiuroids, or brittlestars, occurred on transects in Fagatele Bay, the as yet unidentified specimens we collected during frequent encounters in other localities will add to the few reported from Samoa by Devaney (1974).

--- SENTENCE 789 ---
The high diversity of gastropods in the sanctuary is evident from a comparison of Tables 21 and 22.

--- SENTENCE 790 ---
Of a total of 142 species of gastropods collected at 11 sites around Tutuila, 61 species, or 43% of the total, were also observed or collected in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 791 ---
An additional 36 species were encountered in Fagatele Bay but not elsewhere around the island.

--- SENTENCE 792 ---
When consideration is given to the fact that all sampling in Fagatele Bay was conducted on hard substrates, the relative diversity of gastropods in the sanctuary is even greater.

--- SENTENCE 793 ---
Patterns of succession among corals (Colgan 1981; Pearson 1981) and algae (Biggs and Eminson 1977) have been studied in some detail on reefs devastated by Acanthaster planci, and some changes have been noted in fish communities

(Endean 1973).

--- SENTENCE 794 ---
However, the effects of <u>A</u>.

--- SENTENCE 795 ---
<u>planci</u> infestations on the population structure of benthic macroinvertebrate communities have been esstentially overlooked.

--- SENTENCE 796 ---
Endean (1973) presumed that small organisms associated with corals attacked by the A. planci were killed by the digestive enzymes released by the starfish.

--- SENTENCE 797 ---
Some areas of the Australian Great Barrier Reef were characterized by a marked spread of alcyonaceans following a lapse of about two years after an infestation (Endean and Stablum 1973; Endean 1976).

--- SENTENCE 798 ---
Birkeland (1981) related a report of increased abundance of herbivorous sea urchins following an outbreak of A. planci in Palau before World War II.

--- SENTENCE 799 ---
He further noted the scarcity of urchins at the time of his survey.

--- SENTENCE 800 ---
It is likely that successional changes among macroinvertebrates in Fagatele Bay have taken place already.

--- SENTENCE 801 ---
A previous study of the area reported extensive patches of a whitish sponge in depressions at depths between 15 and 30 feet (U.S. Army Corps of Engineer 1980).

--- SENTENCE 802 ---
Although a few isolated colonies of a whitish sponge were observed during the present study, none was encountered in the area surveyed on transects.

--- SENTENCE 803 ---
One group of macroinvertebrates was conspicuous by its absence in Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 804 ---
The Holothuroidea, or sea cucumbers, comprise a major constituent of the benthic fauna in many Pacific areas (Birkeland 1978; Intes and Menou 1979; Grosenbaugh 1981).

--- SENTENCE 805 ---
However, no holothurians occurred in the 1140-m<sup>2</sup> area surveyed on transects in Fagatele Bay, nor were any sighted on the reef adjacent to transects.

--- SENTENCE 806 ---
Inventories of reef faunas in other areas of Tutuila have recorded a number of holothurian species (Helfrich 1975; U.S. Army Corps of Engineers 1980).

--- SENTENCE 807 ---
Included among the holothurians reported from nearby Pala Lagoon was Actinopyga, a genus that frequently inhabits the reef front.

--- SENTENCE 808 ---
Although

Fagatele Bay would appear to provide suitable habitat for Actinopyga, none was found.

--- SENTENCE 809 ---
Reasons for the absence of holothurians in general, and this genus in particular, at Fagatele Bay are not known.

--- SENTENCE 810 ---
Without a point of reference, it is difficult to assess accurately any changes that may be occurring within the macroinvertebrate community as a result of devastation of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 811 ---
However, with the present study as a baseline, the Fagatele Bay marine sanctuary offers an important opportunity to investigate hypotheses regarding changes in the macroinvertebrate community during the late stages of recovery of the reef.

--- SENTENCE 812 ---
The two predominant invertebrate taxa that we found could have been inferred on the basis of reports from other areas.

--- SENTENCE 813 ---
It will contribute further to our understanding of biological disturbances on coral reefs if macroinvertebrates such as the soft coral <u>Sinularia</u> sp. and the urchin <u>Echinometra mathaei</u> are monitored to measure any changes in their relative densities as the reef undergoes further recovery.

#### Fishes

Fishes observed and counted on-transect and during subsequent 15-20 minute searches are listed in Table 23 for the twelve 30-meter transects conducted within Fagatele Bay.

--- SENTENCE 814 ---
Table 23 also lists additional species observed within the bay during a general reconnaissance dive to 60 ft (18 m).

--- SENTENCE 815 ---
A total of 215 species was observed in the bay.

--- SENTENCE 816 ---
Averages of 25 species and 221 individuals were observed per transect (60 m<sup>2</sup>).

--- SENTENCE 817 ---
When the off-transect fish (those additional species observed within 20 m of the transect line) are included, the average number of species for each area was 62.

--- SENTENCE 818 ---
Greatest diversity occurred at Transect 1-D conducted in the outer portion of the bay near Steps Point at a depth of 40 ft (12 m).

--- SENTENCE 819 ---
A total of 105 on-and off-transect species were observed at this location.

--- SENTENCE 820 ---
Predominant species were the bristle-tooth surgeonfish <u>Ctenochaetus</u> <u>striatus</u> (56% of the on-transect individuals), the damselfish <u>Plectroglyphidodon lacrymatus</u> (7%), the surgeonfish <u>Acanthurus nigrofuscus</u> (4%), the wrasse <u>Thalassoma quinquevittatum</u> (3%), the lined surgeonfish <u>Acanthurus lineatus</u> (2%), the damselfish <u>Chromis vanderbilti</u> (1%), the damselfish <u>Pomacentrus brachialis</u> (1%), and the surgeonfish <u>Acanthurus</u> glaucoparieus (1%).

--- SENTENCE 821 ---
The overwhelming predominance of <u>Ctenochaetus</u> <u>striatus</u> is somewhat deceiving.

--- SENTENCE 822 ---
The vast majority of individuals censused were juveniles (about 3 inches !

--- SENTENCE 823 ---
7-8 cml long) belonging to an exceptionally large year class which had recently settled out of the plankton.

--- SENTENCE 824 ---
Many individuals had shrunken sides and frayed fins.

--- SENTENCE 825 ---
Their unhealthy condition likely resulted in a wholesale dieoff shortly after the survey was conducted.

--- SENTENCE 826 ---
<u>C. striatus</u> was also found by the author to be the most common and abundant species in a very extensive survey of the nearshore waters of Tutuila consisting of sixty-three (63) 100-meter transects conducted over a period of 3 years.

--- SENTENCE 827 ---
However, the species comprised only about 11% of the on-transect fishes in that survey.

--- SENTENCE 828 ---
A figure of that magnitude is probably realistic for Fagatele Bay in the long term.

--- SENTENCE 829 ---
A comparison of the 12 transects by depth reveals an increase in species diversity with depth.

--- SENTENCE 830 ---
Average numbers of on-and off-transect species were: shallow-49, medium-63, and deep-70.

--- SENTENCE 831 ---
Ctenochaetus striatus was clearly the predominant species at all three depths.

--- SENTENCE 832 ---
However, Acanthurus lineatus, Stegastes fasciolatus and Thalassoma quinquevittatum were of second-order predominance in the shallow depths while Plectroglyphidodon lacrymatus was the second most numerous species at 40 ft (12 m).

--- SENTENCE 833 ---
Acanthurus nigrofuscus was very common at all depths.

--- SENTENCE 834 ---
Comparison of the four transects conducted along the exposed outer portion of the bay with the eight transects conducted in the more sheltered inner portions of the bay indicates that species diversity is greater in the exposed portion (average = 70 on-and off-transect species) than in the sheltered portion (average = 58 species).

--- SENTENCE 835 ---
However, fewer individuals were censused in the exposed portion (average = 177) than in the sheltered portion (average = 242).

--- SENTENCE 836 ---
Though clearly the most numerous species in both areas, Ctenochaetus striatus was significantly less prevalent in the exposed areas, (where it comprised 33% of the individuals) than in sheltered areas (64% of the individuals).

--- SENTENCE 837 ---
Thalassoma quinquevittatum was the second-most prevalent species in exposed areas (9%) and Chromis vanderbilti was fourth (5%).

--- SENTENCE 838 ---
In sheltered areas, Plectroglyphidodon lacrymatus (9%) followed C. striatus in predominance.

--- SENTENCE 839 ---
Acanthurus nigrofuscus and Stegastes fasciolatus were common and equally numerous in both areas.

<sup>\*</sup>

--- SENTENCE 1695 ---
Only two Porites lutes were encountered in our samples, but they were both extraordinarily large.

--- SENTENCE 1696 ---
By contacting them, our findings were swamped by the two data.

--- SENTENCE 1697 ---
Both data are included in the calculation of the value in parentheses and one is excluded from other calculations.

--- SENTENCE 1698 ---
Even with only one measurement included in the calculations, the percent cover was estimated at over 100% because the random point landed on the colonies and therefore the distance was small and the area large.

--- SENTENCE 1699 ---
Furthermore, large colonies drape over the substrate and so when their diameters are measured a vertical distance is sometimes incorporated into the measurement.

--- SENTENCE 1700 ---
In cases where large colonies are prevalent, it is thus possible to have more than one square meter of living coral cover per square meter of reef area.

--- SENTENCE 2256 ---
Fishes enumerated or observed during the general survey of inshore fishes at Fagatele Bay conducted 5-12 April 1985.

--- SENTENCE 2257 ---
The presence of a particular species is denoted by the number of individuals censused during the transect or a "p" which indicated the species was observed within 10 m of the transect during a subsequent 20-minute search.

--- SENTENCE 2258 ---
Transect 1-S originated at the shallow stake of Transect 1; Transect 1-M originated at the middle stake of Transect 1; Transect 1-D originated at the deep stake of Transect 1; etc.

--- SENTENCE 2259 ---
The column labled "Bay" lists additional species observed within the bay during a general reconnaissance dive to 60 ft (18 m).

#### CHANGES in CORAL COMMUNITIES

#### FOLLOWING the OUTBREAK of Acanthaster planci 1978-1979

Two of us (Birkeland and Randall) visited Tutuila in April at 3-year intervals, in 1979, 1982 and 1985.

--- SENTENCE 2452 ---
In April 1979, coral communities in Masefau Bay, at northwest Aunuu Island, and at Matuli Point (Fig. 2), were being heavily preyed upon by <u>Acanthaster planci</u> which was present by the thousands.

--- SENTENCE 2453 ---
In Aoa Bay, Onenoa Bay, Fagasa Bay, and Cape Larsen (Fig. 2), the coral communities were obviously preyed upon heavily a short time previous to our visit.

--- SENTENCE 2454 ---
The coral community at Fatu Rock may have been preyed upon several months earlier in deeper water, but appeared to have been bypassed by <u>A</u>.

--- SENTENCE 2455 ---
<u>planci</u> in the shallow zone of turbulant water.

--- SENTENCE 2456 ---
The coral communities at Fagafue Bay and Massacre Bay (Fig. 2) had not been preyed upon extensively.

--- SENTENCE 2457 ---
We returned in April 1982 and 1985 to assess the changes in community structure of hermatypic corals at these locations (Tables 4-15).

--- SENTENCE 2458 ---
The average abundances (number/m²) of coral colonies at each site, depth and year are given in Table 24.

--- SENTENCE 2459 ---
The locations were selected a priori with the goal of obtaining data for a 3-factor analysis, the factors being wave-exposure, depth and time (Table 25).

--- SENTENCE 2460 ---
Six areas generally sheltered from wave action were paired with six other nearby areas exposed to open coast conditions, with inside Masefau Bay: outside Masefau Bay, Aoa Bay: Onenoa Bay, northwest Aunuu Island: Matuli Point, Fagasa Bay: Cape Larsen, Fagafue Bay: Massacre Bay, and Rainmaker Hotel:

--- SENTENCE 2461 ---
Fatu Rock being the protected: exposed pairs of sites.

--- SENTENCE 2462 ---
There was a significant difference (Table 25) in abundance of corals between years (Table 24).

--- SENTENCE 2463 ---
As would be expected, corals generally were more abundant six years after the outbreak of <u>Acanthaster planci</u> (1985) than they were three years after (1982).

--- SENTENCE 2464 ---
The exception were in shallow water (2-3 m)

at Fagasa Bay and at Cape Larsen and in deeper water (6 m) at the Rainmaker Hotel and at Fatu Rock.

--- SENTENCE 2481 ---
We suggest the exceptions in shallow water possibly may be related to fishing with Clorox and the exceptions at Pago Pago may be related to urban and industrial pollution.

--- SENTENCE 2482 ---
When Birkeland visited Tutuila in July 1980, he observed several areas in shallow water between Sita Bay and Fagasa (Fig. 2) where numerous white swaths laid as bands straight across neighboring colonies.

--- SENTENCE 2483 ---
Acanthaster planci does not feed in such a pattern.

--- SENTENCE 2484 ---
This pattern could have been a result of a toxic chemical washing across the colonies in a surge of water.

--- SENTENCE 2485 ---
This area of damage to coral apparently from Clorox is also the region in which there was a slight decrease in abundance of corals during a period when corals were increasing in abundance in other areas.

--- SENTENCE 2486 ---
When we visited Tutuila in 1985, the oily brown plume of water from Pago Pago could be seen passing Fatu Rock.

--- SENTENCE 2487 ---
During our stay in Tutuila in 1979, Pago Pago Bay did not appear to be nearly so polluted as it was in 1985.

--- SENTENCE 2488 ---
The striking decrease in coral abundance at 6 m depth on the reef at the Rainmaker Hotel and the slight decrease in coral abundance at Fatu Rock may result from increased turbidity and perhaps even toxic effects of polluted harbor waters.

--- SENTENCE 2489 ---
The abundance of corals varied also with depth (3 or 6 m) and with whether the area was exposed to or protected from wave assault (Tables 24 and 25).

--- SENTENCE 2490 ---
Corals were generally more abundant in shallow water and this difference in abundance was greatest in areas exposed to heavy wave action (Table 24).

--- SENTENCE 2491 ---
The interaction between depth and exposure to wave action was also significant (Table 25).

--- SENTENCE 2492 ---
This may be attributed to occasional protection of reef communities in shallow water from predation by Acanthaster planci.

--- SENTENCE 2493 ---
A. planci seems to have difficulty maintaining its position in an area with strong wave action.

--- SENTENCE 2494 ---
Therefore, an aggregation of A. planci tends to have a greater effect

on coral communities at depths below the surf zone.

--- SENTENCE 2495 ---
This difference in effects of <u>A</u>.

--- SENTENCE 2496 ---
<u>planci</u> is probably greater on exposed coasts than in protected bays.

--- SENTENCE 2497 ---
Although the effects of depth and degree of wave exposure are both significant (Table 25), the interactions between these factors are also significant.

--- SENTENCE 2498 ---
Location at coastal sites around Tutuila is a significant factor for abundances of coral colonies per  $m^2$  (Table 24 and 25), for percent cover of reef substrate by hermatypic corals (Tables 26 and 27), and for mean colony diameter (Table 28 and 29).

--- SENTENCE 2499 ---
This is not surprising because locations differ in past history, i.e., in whether coral communities were heavily preyed upon by  $\underline{A}$ .

--- SENTENCE 2500 ---
planci within the last decade and probably also in abundance and success of coral recruitment.

--- SENTENCE 2501 ---
Although the abundance of coral colonies and substrate surface cover by hermatypic corals was greatly reduced, a few small living colonies or portions of colonies of each coral species apparently did remain tucked away in crevices or depressions.

--- SENTENCE 2502 ---
However, the diversity of the coral community often increased during recovery of the reef community (Table 30).

--- SENTENCE 2503 ---
This must be largely a result of an increase in evenness of representation by different species and a decrease in predominance of a few species (Table 30) rather than a result of an increase in the number of species.

--- SENTENCE 2504 ---
We are tempted to hypothesize that the increase in prevalence of Millepora at the reefs near the Rainmaker Hotel is a result of greater tolerance of Millepora to pollution.

--- SENTENCE 2505 ---
This matter should be followed by observing further changes in coral community structure in Pago Pago Bay.

### INFLUENCE of <u>Acanthaster-Induced</u> CORAL KILLS on FISH COMMUNITIES

--- SENTENCE 2658 ---
AT FAGATELE BAY and at CAPE LARSEN

During 1978 and 1979, a population explosion of <u>Acanthaster planci</u> killed a large proportion of the reef corals around Tutuila Island.

--- SENTENCE 2659 ---
An estimated 80-90% of the corals were killed on the reef fronts of Fagatele Bay and Cape Larsen during October 1978.

--- SENTENCE 2660 ---
Fish transects censused before and after the coral kills in these two areas facilitate analysis of the impact of this perturbation on the fish communities

Fishes were censused three times at Cape Larsen prior to the coral kill and two times subsequently, including the census done in April 1985.

--- SENTENCE 2661 ---
The results of these transects are listed in Table 31.

--- SENTENCE 2662 ---
Fishes were censused once at Fagatele Bay just prior to the coral kill and again in April 1985.

--- SENTENCE 2663 ---
These results are listed in Table 32 along with the results of two censuses conducted at Sita Bay over the same time period.

--- SENTENCE 2664 ---
In contrast to the other two areas, the corals at Sita Bay were largely untouched by Acanthaster planci.

--- SENTENCE 2665 ---
Sita Bay, therefore, serves as a control.

--- SENTENCE 2666 ---
Differences between its two censuses are a measure of population changes resulting from factors other than an extensive coral kill, as well as a measure of data variability and the precision of the method.

--- SENTENCE 2667 ---
The latter two factors can also be examined through comparison of the first three columns of Table 31 depicting the results of Cape Larsen censuses conducted only four days apart (a period of time too short to result in any real population changes) and ten weeks apart.

--- SENTENCE 2668 ---
The unusually large numbers of <u>Ctenochaetus</u> <u>striatus</u> in April 1985 again confuse the analysis.

--- SENTENCE 2669 ---
In each of the three study areas, the total number of all fishes censused in 1985 exceeds the totals for each of the previous

TABLE 31.

--- SENTENCE 2670 ---
Fishes enumerated or observed during repetitive transects conducted at Cape Larsen before and after an extensive coral kill by Acanthaster planci late in 1978.

--- SENTENCE 2671 ---
The columns are headed by transect dates.

censuses.

--- SENTENCE 2819 ---
When the numbers of  $\underline{C}$ .

--- SENTENCE 2820 ---
striatus are subtracted from each total, however, the 1985 totals are each less than the earlier totals for the same area.

--- SENTENCE 2821 ---
The number of transects conducted before and after the Acanthaster infestation are relatively few and the data are highly variable so only a few conclusions can be drawn with any degree of confidence.

--- SENTENCE 2822 ---
As with the corals, it appears that the total number of fish was little affected by the coral kill.

--- SENTENCE 2823 ---
The total number of species in the Cape Larsen censuses averaged 99 for the three censuses before the kill and 104 for the two censuses after the kill.

--- SENTENCE 2824 ---
At Fagatele Bay, the total dipped from 113 to 107, but the decrease at Sita Bay (the control) from 96 to 88 was even more drastic, so the Fagatele decrease cannot attributed to the coral kill.

--- SENTENCE 2825 ---
A few gross changes in species numbers are evident from the tables and are likely to be results of the <u>Acanthaster</u> population explosion and subsequent coral devastation.

--- SENTENCE 2826 ---
Clearly, the numbers of the damselfishes <u>Plectroglyphidodon dickii</u>, and probably <u>Plectroglyphidodon johnstonianus</u>, have been reduced.

--- SENTENCE 2827 ---
Both are known to associate with and feed on <u>Acropora</u> corals.

--- SENTENCE 2828 ---
There is some indication that a few of the surgeonfish species have increased, although the basis for this conclusion is clouded by the unusually large population of juvenile <u>Ctenochaetus striatus</u>.

--- SENTENCE 2829 ---
An increase in surgeonfish populations might be expected as most species of this family feed on benthic algae which grows on dead coral substrate.

--- SENTENCE 2830 ---
The damselfish <u>Plectroglyphidodon lacrymatus</u> seems also to have increased significantly after the coral kill.

--- SENTENCE 2831 ---
It, too, is known to feed primarily on benthic algae.

--- SENTENCE 2832 ---
Overall, the impact of the extensive coral kill on the fish communities of Tutuila Island appears to be surprisingly small.

--- SENTENCE 2833 ---
Species diversity and numbers of individuals remain high.

--- SENTENCE 2834 ---
Although there are no data relative to

fish biomass, in the subjective opinion of the author, that too is probably unchanged.

--- SENTENCE 2835 ---
Fishes targeted by Samoan fishermen appear as plentiful and as large as ever.

--- SENTENCE 2836 ---
#### Acknowledgments

Bill Thomas, Assistant Project Manager, Sanctuary Programs Division, NOAA, was the catalyst for this project.

--- SENTENCE 2837 ---
He contacted us to inform us of the program, designed the objectives in the Statement of Work, and arranged for the creation and funding of the program.

--- SENTENCE 2838 ---
This project would not have been accomplished if he were not involved.

--- SENTENCE 2839 ---
The cooperation of the Office of Marine Resources, Government of American Samoa, was superb.

--- SENTENCE 2840 ---
Despite demands on their boats and equipment for other projects, they were always able to accommodate us, and we were able to work in the field every day during our 2-week stay.

--- SENTENCE 2841 ---
Mr. Raymond Tulafono, Director of the Office of Marine Resources (OMR), gave us full support and provided use of their 28-ft catamaran MASIMASI for all our work at Fagatele Bay and use of their Zodiac, outboard motor, truck and trailer for work at other sites around the island.

--- SENTENCE 2842 ---
Their scuba air compressor provided us with 143 air fills during our 2 weeks of intensive field work.

--- SENTENCE 2843 ---
Mr. Raymond Buckley, Chief Fishery Biologist at OMR, was instrumental in interweaving our use in boats with their schedule of use so that all projects were accomplished and nobody was put on hold.

--- SENTENCE 2844 ---
Ray Buckley, David Itano and Fa'asega "Stu" Kuresa also gave us substantial field help in boat operation, laying out and retrieving fish transect lines, and taking depth contour readings.

--- SENTENCE 2845 ---
Dave and Stu even helped us with field work on weekends and on Good Friday.

--- SENTENCE 2846 ---
David Itano also made a particular effort to observe and practise our survey and sampling techniques in order to provide continuity to the monitoring of the transects after we have left.

--- SENTENCE 2847 ---
It is when we get such full cooperation from other institutions on projects like this that we feel marine science is a profession with a particular comraderie.

--- SENTENCE 2848 ---
Ideia Sackryas diligently drafted the maps and figures for our report.

--- SENTENCE 2849 ---
This was not an easy job because the aerial photographs were not directly vertical.

--- SENTENCE 2850 ---
Therefore locations had to be determined and distances had to be checked by triangulation.

--- SENTENCE 2851 ---
We are also grateful to Marie Peredo and Diane Fuller for patiently typing this manuscript and the 149 pages of tabular material.

--- SENTENCE 2852 ---
Mei Tsu volunteered to log the data into the IBM PC for analysis and proofread much of the tabular material.
